78       #buildingAlberta                                                                                                  GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
       MANAGEMENT’S RESPONSIBILITY FOR REPORTING



       Responsibility for the integrity and objectivity of the non-financial performance results reported in the
       province’s annual performance report Measuring Up, a component of the Province of Alberta Annual
       Report, rests with the government. Measuring Up is prepared by the Assistant Deputy Minister of Budget
       Development and Reporting under the general direction of the Deputy Minister of Treasury Board and
       Finance as authorized by the President of the Treasury Board and Minister of Finance pursuant to Section
       10 of the Government Accountability Act in effect March 31, 2013.
       Under Section 10, the government had to prepare and make public on or before June 30 of each year
       a consolidated annual report for the Province of Alberta for the fiscal year ended on the preceding
       March 31. The annual report must include a comparison of the actual performance results to the targets
       included in the government business plan and an explanation of any significant variances. As required by
       the Government Accountability Act, Measuring Up is published with the consolidated annual report of the
       Government of Alberta that forms part of the Public Accounts.
       In order to meet government reporting requirements, the Assistant Deputy Minister of Budget
       Development and Reporting obtains performance results relating to each ministry as necessary. Deputy
       heads in each ministry are responsible for maintaining management and internal control systems to
       ensure that the information provided by their ministry for presentation in Measuring Up is prepared in
       accordance with the following criteria:
       ■■   Reliability— information used in applying performance measure methodologies agrees with
            underlying source data for the current and prior years’ results.
       ■■   Understandability—the performance measure methodologies and results are presented clearly.
       ■■   Comparability—the methodologies for performance measure preparation are applied consistently for
            the current and prior years’ results.
       ■■   Completeness—goals, performance measures and related targets match those included in Budget
            2012.
       Measuring Up is reviewed by the Audit Committee, which is established under the Auditor General
       Act. The Audit Committee advises the Lieutenant Governor in Council on the scope and results of the
       Auditor General’s audit of Measuring Up. The Auditor General of Alberta provides an independent
       report on the verification of completeness, reliability, comparability and understandability of 10 of 32
       performance measures published in Measuring Up, which are prepared by the government. The 10 audited
       performance measures are identified in Measuring Up with (audited). Annually, Measuring Up is tabled
       in the Legislature as a part of the Public Accounts and is referred to the Standing Committee on Public
       Accounts of the Legislative Assembly.
       Performance measures for each goal were selected for audit based on a combination of characteristics
       including: best represented the goal and mandated initiatives; had well established methodology and
       reporting of data; and had outcomes over which the government had a greater degree of influence.

       Original signed by
       Annette Trimbee
       Deputy Minister of Treasury Board and Finance
       June 19, 2013



MEASURING UP                                                                       #buildingAlberta             79
     READERS’ GUIDE
     Measuring Up is part of the Alberta government’s commitment to be open and accountable to the
     people of Alberta. It endeavours to go beyond the reporting of financial information by answering
     the questions: what did the government achieve with the dollars spent and did the government make
     progress toward achieving its goals?
     Answering these questions is accomplished by comparing the results of performance measures to
     targets and reporting on progress made on key priorities identified under the seven goals of the
     2012‑15 Government of Alberta Strategic Plan. When comparing targets to results, readers will notice
     that sometimes targets do not correspond directly with a measure’s previous or current results. Targets
     may be set before these results are known or are based, not on projected results, but instead, for
     example, on public expectations or comparisons to the results that other organizations have achieved.
     Measuring Up contains results analysis by goal, results-based budgeting update reports, performance
     measure report tables for each goal, the Auditor’s Report on selected performance measures and
     sources and notes. This year’s Measuring Up also provides some forward looking information to set the
     context for the government’s overall strategic direction and foreshadow the work we are continuing to
     do to achieve the outcomes that Albertans want.
     These outcomes are part of the government’s Building Alberta Plan: investing in families and
     communities, including new roads, schools and health facilities; living within the province’s means
     by challenging every dollar the government spends and making sure every program continues to
     deliver real results for people; and building new markets for the province’s resources, obtaining the
     fairest price and protecting the jobs and prosperity Albertans depend on. Government is putting the
     Building Alberta Plan into action through a number of different initiatives which are outlined in the
     annual report.
     To achieve its outcomes, the government has identified where special attention, discipline and effort
     are required to drive meaningful change and results. They are:
     ■■    early childhood development;
     ■■    primary health care;
     ■■    education and entrepreneurship;
     ■■    re-balancing the fiscal framework;
     ■■    an integrated resource management system; and,
     ■■    expanded market access for Alberta products.

     WORKING TOGETHER
     The Alberta Public Service (APS) delivers government programs and services that matter to Albertans.
     By living our values, embracing innovation and proudly working together, APS members are driving
     a transformation in the way we work, positioning our organization for success, and delivering
     responsible change for Albertans.
     Given the complexity and change inherent in our environment now and into the future, it is essential
     that we continue to review and renew our leadership practices, learning and engagement skills,
     business processes and communication with each other – in essence, “how we work” – to ensure
     excellence right across the APS.

82              #buildingAlberta                                      GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
THEME

        1           INVESTING IN FAMILIES AND COMMUNITIES: Supporting healthy and strong families and
                    communities is an investment in Albertans and Alberta’s future


               Three of the government’s seven strategic goals fall under the Investing in Families and
               Communities theme:
               ■■   Strategic Goal One: Honour Alberta’s Communities
               ■■   Strategic Goal Two: Support Vulnerable Albertans
               ■■   Strategic Goal Three: Healthy Albertans

               There are many successes to celebrate for 2012-13. From growing Alberta’s tourism industry to
               supporting community-based crime prevention projects, the Government of Alberta remains
               committed to investing in Alberta’s families and communities. As part of this commitment, the
               government is reviewing programs and services aimed at supporting healthy and strong families and
               communities to ensure it is achieving the outcomes that Albertans want.

STRATEGIC GOAL ONE. Honour Alberta’s Communities: Our communities are safe, prosperous,
welcoming, culturally diverse and desirable places to live or destinations to visit.


                2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Municipal Sustainability Initiative
               Municipalities need stable funding in order to provide essential local services to Albertans. The
               Municipal Sustainability Initiative (MSI) provides significant financial support to municipalities for
               infrastructure and is the province’s key initiative for strengthening the municipal sector.
               Under this initiative, government has maintained infrastructure grant funding levels to
               municipalities. Municipalities received $849 million in MSI capital funding in 2012-13.
               In 2012, in response to the minister’s mandate to review and assess the MSI, program changes were

MEASURING UP                                                                                                 #buildingAlberta                    83
     Tourism Development
     In 2012-13, the tourism levy totalled almost $82 million, an increase of approximately 13 per
     cent compared to 2011-12. This increase is indicative of greater levels of activity by Albertans
     and other visitors travelling around the province using accommodations like hotels, motels, bed
     and breakfasts or similar establishments. The province, other levels of government, partners and
     industry worked to grow tourism by addressing the need for the development and marketing of
     tourism experiences, products and destinations across the province.
     For example, the government and the tourism industry supported an innovative, collaborative
     tourism model piloted by the Canadian Badlands. This not-for-profit corporation represents the
     interests of 65 municipalities and is integrating tourism destination development, management
     and marketing across the 90,000 square kilometre region in southeast Alberta. Support was also
     provided for developing a world-class trail head at Canmore Visitor Information Centre by linking
     it to the Rocky Mountain Legacy Trail. Planned work will create a four-season destination with
     opportunities for cycling, walking or running at the recreational and competitive level.
     Through sport tourism events, the province attracts visitors, raises awareness of Alberta as a
     sport destination, generates new tourism business and increases international recognition for the
     province’s active communities, people and natural landscapes. The Alberta government piloted the
     viability of using competitive sport tourism to maximize its contribution to Alberta’s economy at
     the International Ski Federation Cross Country World Cup in Canmore. In total, the government
     provided $895,000 in support of the Alberta World Cup 2012. This included $200,000 from
     Travel Alberta to the Alberta World Cup Society to produce the opening sequence broadcast
     presenting Alberta’s communities, people and natural landscapes to the thousands of spectators
     in Canmore and reaching an estimated television audience of over 30 million viewers around
     the world. Support included Canmore Business and Tourism receiving funding to launch a new
     Canmore Nordic Festival adding a festival atmosphere and showcasing Aboriginal performers and
     local artists as well as culinary experiences provided by local restaurants.

     Safe Communities (SafeCom)
     Alberta’s Crime Prevention Framework takes a broad, integrated approach to creating safe
     communities which balances prevention, treatment and enforcement. The main areas of focus
     under SafeCom have been gang reduction, marijuana grow operations, Aboriginal justice,
     addressing mental health issues in the justice system, innovative legislation, and integrated justice
     (the integration of case-management, rehabilitative, treatment and support services aimed at
     reducing re-offending).

     Safe Communities Innovation Fund
     The $60 million Safe Communities Innovation Fund (SCIF) was established in 2008 and has
     funded a total of 88 community-based crime prevention pilot projects. SCIF was discontinued in
     Budget 2013 but existing SCIF grant agreements are being honoured.

STRATEGIC GOAL TWO. Support Vulnerable Albertans: Our most vulnerable Albertans are protected and
supported so they can reach their full potential.

                2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Social Policy Framework
               In 2012, the government invited Albertans to share their ideas about how social policy can achieve
               better outcomes for children, families, individuals and communities. Public engagement processes
               provided Albertans with opportunities online and at community-led discussions to help create a
               framework that would guide decisions about the relevance and effectiveness of social policies and
               programs.
               On February 28, 2013, Premier Redford was joined by Cabinet members, community and business
               leaders, and stakeholders to adopt Alberta’s Social Policy Framework. The framework will guide
               government and community partners to better address social challenges such as poverty, addiction,
               family violence and child care. It supports transformational change and envisions a renewal of social
               policy with a system-wide focus on shared responsibility, collaboration and transparency.
               Over the course of six months in 2012, more than 31,000 Albertans participated in a two-phase
               engagement process that brought together Aboriginal Elders, business leaders, communities, non-
               profit organizations, service delivery agencies, municipalities, elected officials, and Government of
               Alberta staff.




               S:\TBF\BusFunctions\SMP\BP-ZTOPICS\Measuring Up\Excel Files\
               Goal One 2013.xlsx /Goal-1_07_Property_Crime-CLR             printed: 6/17/2013/5:25 PM


MEASURING UP                                                                                                    #buildingAlberta                85
     A report on the public engagement, Summing Up: Albertans’ Perspectives for a Social Policy
     Framework is available to read on the main Social Policy Framework site at
     www.socialpolicy.alberta.ca.
     The intended outcomes of the Social Policy Framework are for all Albertans to be:
     ■■   Safe—Live free from fear of abuse and violence;
     ■■   Healthy—Achieve the highest attainable standards of health and well-being.
     ■■   Secure and Resilient—Support themselves and their households through safe work and career
          opportunities, with access to effective income supports when in financial need;
     ■■   Lifelong Learners—Develop the knowledge, skills, and commitment to learning necessary to
          realize their potential and participate in society;
     ■■   Included—Feel welcomed in the communities where they live, learn, and work; and
     ■■   Active and Engaged—Explore opportunities to participate in recreational activities and cultural
          experiences, and to engage in society.

     The framework will evolve over time. While it is intended as a tool to guide government and
     community efforts in social policy for the next 10 to 15 years, it is also meant to be a subject of
     open dialogue to ensure that it responds to social change as it occurs.

     Information Sharing Strategy (ISS)
     Sharing information both internally in government and with service agencies is necessary to make
     effective and efficient decisions for the health, education and safety of children and families. The
     purpose of ISS is to provide the best outcomes for children and families through collaboration and
     the controlled sharing of personal and health information.
     Development of the strategy is complete and implementation planning is underway. The
     objectives of the strategy are that information will be shared when reasonable and necessary unless
     specifically prohibited; client information will be shared as appropriate across the GoA and with
     external organizations to support effectiveness and efficiency; accountability for information
     management will be in place and understood by all parties; risk around sharing information will be
     acknowledged and managed within the human services sector and that information will be shared
     while respecting privacy.
     Phase one of the strategy’s implementation, which is expected to take two years, covers the
     ministries of Human Services, Health and Education, and their respective service delivery partners.
     Phase two, which is expected to take another three years, will extend to other relevant government
     ministries and stakeholders.
     The government consulted with stakeholders on how barriers to information-sharing impact
     their capacity to make the best decisions for children and families. Summaries of the consultation
     discussions are available online at www.socialpolicy.alberta.ca.

     Assured Income for the Severely Handicapped (AISH)
     AISH provides financial and health-related assistance to eligible adults with a disability. The
     disability must be permanent and substantially limit the person’s ability to earn a living. The
     monthly average caseload for the AISH program was over 45,000 during the 2012-13 fiscal year.
     In 2012, government committed to increasing the monthly living allowance and employment
     income exemptions for clients of the AISH program. Effective April 1, 2012, the maximum AISH
     financial benefit was increased by $400 per month from $1,188 to $1,588. The thresholds for the
     employment income exemptions were also doubled from $400 to $800 per month for single clients

86   #buildingAlberta                                          GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
               and from $975 to $1,950 per month for AISH clients with co-habiting partners and/or dependent
               children.
               Increased exemptions for employment income allow individuals to work to the extent they are able
               to while maintaining the support they receive through AISH. Participation in the workforce not
               only increases income, but facilitates independence and builds connections to the community.

               Income Support
              The purpose of the Income Support program is to help Albertans be as independent as possible,
              move to employment and stay in the labour force. The program’s success at achieving this has been
              relatively stable over the last several years. Prior education, work experience and a range of barriers
              affecting participants are all important factors impacting the program’s success. Those that stopped
              receiving Income Support without obtaining employment often have transitioned to training
              programs, entered new partnerships or received support from other sources (CPP, EI and disability-
              related income). Many participants in Income Support face multiple barriers and require more
PDF name: MU12_ex2_G3-chrt05_lowIncome-clr.pdf
              support to succeed in long-term attachment to the labour market.                    2012-13 Measuring Up

STRATEGIC GOAL THREE. Healthy Albertans: A health system where Albertans have the supports they
need to lead healthy lives.

                2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Primary Health Care
               The Alberta government has several initiatives underway to improve primary health care. In April
               2012, three pilot Family Care Clinics (FCCs) opened in Edmonton, Calgary, and Slave Lake.
               Initial evaluations of the three FCC sites indicate approximately 4,000 previously unattached
               Albertans are now attached to a FCC. All FCC clients have access to interdisciplinary teams to
               better manage their care.

             model. Government began work with the Primary Care Alliance and Alberta Health Services to
             develop a plan for the future evolution of Primary Care Networks, to provide individuals with a
             more standard and broader range of services from Primary Care Networks.
              The Health Minister’s Advisory Committee on Primary Health Care includes stakeholders from
              major health professional associations and colleges and facilitates ongoing consultations with key

             Seniors and Continuing Care
             Continuing Care Centres are congregate living accommodations which will provide multiple
             levels of care and accommodation in one setting. The focus of these centres is on individuals and
             adjusting the health care, personal care and accommodation services to meet their changing needs
             and help them maintain an exceptional quality of life while aging-in-place. This innovative model
             will reduce or prevent seniors and persons with disabilities from having to move to another facility
             if their care needs change. Continuing Care Centre demonstration sites in Calgary and Red Deer
             are expected to be operational in early 2014.
             Government maintained its commitment to expand continuing care spaces. Since March 2010,
             over 3,000 spaces have been added, leaving about 2,300 remaining to achieve the 5-year target of
             5,300 by March 2015.
             Chiropractic services were added to Alberta Seniors’ health benefit coverage. Those enrolled in
             the Coverage for Seniors health benefit plan receive $25 per visit towards chiropractic services, to a
             maximum of $200 per annual benefit period.
             Income exemption and thresholds were adjusted in June 2012 for the Alberta Seniors Benefit
             program. As a result, qualifying income thresholds were raised to ensure seniors’ benefits are not
             negatively affected by increases to federal seniors’ benefits.




88           #buildingAlberta                                                         GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
               Passed by the Alberta Legislature in March 2012, the Seniors Property Tax Deferral Act facilitated
               the introduction of the Seniors Property Tax Deferral Program which allows senior homeowners to
               apply for a low-interest loan through the Alberta government to defer all or part of their property
               taxes. The program began in May 2013.

               Addictions and Mental Health
               In October 2012, two 20-bed units were opened at Alberta Hospital Edmonton; Young Adult
               Evaluation, Treatment and Reintegration Services serving 17 to 24 year olds transitioning to adult
               services from child and adolescent services and Alternate Level of Care Transition Services serving
               adult patients from acute care psychiatry in-patient units in the Edmonton Zone.
               The province announced a new Tobacco Reduction Strategy for 2012-22 to further protect young
               Albertans from tobacco and provide more support to help Albertans quit using tobacco products.
               The strategy serves as the provincial strategic framework for tobacco control for the next 10 years.
               Government contributed $5 million to enhance existing addiction and mental health services for
               homeless Albertans in Edmonton, Calgary and Lethbridge as part of the Addiction and Mental
               Health Strategy.
               The province expanded mental health and campus addiction services for post-secondary students
               at the University of Alberta, University of Calgary and University of Lethbridge. Funding was
               announced to support developing models of care that can be used on campuses across Alberta.

               Pharmaceuticals
               In Alberta, the costs for generic drugs have been reduced significantly in recent years. Further
               changes to generic drug pricing became effective July 1, 2012, when the Alberta government
               reduced the price it pays for all generic drugs from 45 per cent to 35 per cent of the comparable
               brand drug price.

               Health Promotion and Disease Prevention
               The province expanded their immunization program in July 2012 to include a second dose of the
               chickenpox vaccine for children between the ages of four and six. In 2012-13, Albertans had more
               health professionals and more locations offering influenza immunization than ever before. Almost
               900,000 Albertans were immunized over a six-week period. The number of pharmacists and other
               community partners offering the influenza vaccine to Albertans doubled in 2012-13.
               As of July 1, 2012, changes were made to benefits under the Alberta Monitoring for Health
               Program. The benefit for low-income insulin-treated Albertans under age 65 was increased and
               a new benefit was added for Alberta women with insulin-treated gestational diabetes, who have
               no insurance coverage for basic diabetes supplies. Also, basic diabetic supply coverage for 7,700
               insulin-treated seniors who were Alberta Monitoring for Health Program clients was transferred to
               the Coverage for Seniors plan. The $600 in diabetic supplies coverage was also extended to eligible
               members of the Non-Group Coverage and Palliative Care Drug Coverage plans (administered by
               Alberta Blue Cross).
               Government continues to investigate and respond to public health threats, ensuring that Albertans’
               health is protected. In 2012, the province collaborated with Alberta Health Services on over 800
               disease outbreaks. Throughout the province, norovirus and influenza were the most common causes
               of outbreaks.




MEASURING UP                                                                          #buildingAlberta             89
              Infrastructure
              The Alberta government continued to invest in health infrastructure, with improvements to
              regional hospitals and new facilities offering specialized services in Edmonton and Calgary.
              The South Health Campus in Calgary, the largest hospital in Alberta’s history, has been opening
              in phases since mid 2012. The South Health Campus will include a broad range of in-patient and
              out-patient services, with a focus on wellness services and facilities. These wellness services will
              make illness prevention, management and community health education an important focus of the
              campus.
              A new cancer care centre, to be located on the Foothills Medical Centre grounds, was announced
              in March 2013. Targeted to begin construction in 2015-16, the new centre will include in-patient
              beds, the latest advanced cancer diagnostic and treatment technologies, an out-patient facility, and
              dedicated research space to provide more opportunities for cancer innovation.
              The world-class Kaye Edmonton Clinic opened in December 2012, giving people living in
              northern Alberta improved patient care, and access to specialized services and technologies.
            The province approved plans for redevelopment projects at the Medicine Hat and Chinook regional
            hospitals, which will improve access to health care services for area residents. ConstructionMeasuring

                 Individuals waiting in the community for continuing care are assessed based on their needs and are more difficult

90            #buildingAlberta                                                             GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
                    Results-Based Budgeting Lines of Business−Investing in Families and Communities

               As the government moves through the results-based budgeting (RBB) process, opportunities are
               being realized to shape programs and services and align resources in the most effective and efficient
               way. The RBB process allows government to identify unnecessary spending and programs that
               have outlived their usefulness, providing an opportunity to redirect dollars to help achieve higher
               priority outcomes.
               As indicated in Figure 1 on page 81 there are seven lines of business that align with the Investing in
               Families and Communities theme:
               ■■    Municipalities and Regional Planning
               ■■    Recreation and Culture
               ■■    Protecting Albertans
               ■■    Supporting Albertans in Need
               ■■    Early Childhood Development
               ■■    Health
               ■■    Wellness

               Municipalities and Regional Planning
               This line of business includes programs and services that support community planning and
               municipal infrastructure and the provision of direct financial support to municipalities.
               The review of the municipalities and regional planning line of business is nearing completion. The
               28 programs and services under review focus on the government’s collaboration with municipalities
               and communities through financial, advisory and administrative supports. The support ranges from
               enhancing the long-term viability and sustainability of municipalities and communities, including
               Metis Settlements and building and maintaining infrastructure, to the adherence to safety codes
               and standards for homes, buildings and structures such as boilers and elevators.
               The results-based budgeting process revealed a number of opportunities to improve outcomes for
               Albertans including further streamlining municipal grant programs. The review also indicated
               that implementation of long-term governance and funding arrangements with Metis Settlements
               will lead to better socio-economic outcomes for Metis Settlement members, improvements to the
               delivery of essential services, and increased long-term sustainability of Metis Settlements.

               Recreation and Culture
               This line of business includes programs and services supporting active living and community
               participation as well as those that support the arts, historic sites, recreation and sports. Through the
               results-based budgeting review process thus far, an opportunity was identified to better align and
               coordinate government processes relating to sporting events. In addition, there are opportunities
               for strengthening relationships and linkages between sport, tourism, culture and other government
               sectors (local, provincial, federal) when planning and executing these events. One of the desired
               outcomes is to enhance the profile and appeal of Alberta as the host destination and attract external
               visitation and expenditures to the province. For example, sport tourism is one of the fastest growing
               segments of the Canadian tourism industry with travellers spending about $3.6 billion each year
               nationally.




MEASURING UP                                                                            #buildingAlberta            91
     Protecting Albertans
     This line of business includes programs and services that protect Albertans and support safe
     communities. Programs and services in this line of business are scheduled for review in 2013-14.
     The results-based budgeting review process will examine relevance, effectiveness and efficiency in
     achieving overall outcomes for protecting Albertans.

     Support to Albertans in Need
     This line of business includes programs and services to support Alberta’s most vulnerable, including
     people with developmental or other disabilities, provide income or housing supports, or support
     individual capacity. The results-based budgeting review of this line of business clearly demonstrates
     the need to allocate resources to those most in need and ensure that the dollars spent have the
     greatest positive impact possible. A single comprehensive disability support system where supports
     are combined within a single ministry is the best structure to achieve positive outcomes for clients.
     An integrated approach which takes a holistic view of the person and assesses the needs and
     strengths of the individual when determining the level of supports government will provide helps
     to ensure that the right support is provided at the right time.
     Government assessed disability support programs, not just individually, but collectively as a system
     of supports. Programs were reviewed according to their relevance to the Social Policy Framework,
     their effectiveness in achieving desired outcomes, and efficiency in delivering the greatest possible
     impact from resources invested. A comprehensive set of outcomes was also developed for the
     system of disability supports including client outcomes, program delivery outcomes, and system
     design outcomes.
     The Support to Albertans in Need line of business is a major part of the Social Policy Framework
     previously reported on page 85–86.

     Early Childhood Development
     This line of business includes programs and services focused on enriched early learning and child
     care environments, parenting resources and improved maternal and infant health. The results-
     based budgeting process revealed insights to help move Alberta’s Approach to Early Childhood
     Development forward. The review process resulted in recommendations that will further advance
     the one-government approach of a cohesive, inclusive and responsive early childhood development
     system.
     Improving early childhood development is a good investment. Intervening earlier rather than
     later increases the positive impact on a child’s brain development and life course outcomes. This
     potentially reduces the need for government supports as children grow into adults.

     Health
     This line of business includes programs and services providing acute care, continuing care, primary
     care and health benefits to Albertans as well as those that support rehabilitation and home care,
     assisted and supportive living, health facilities planning and infrastructure, medical equipment
     purchases and cancer research. The results-based budgeting review process undertaken in 2012-13
     for programs and services in the primary health care area found that, in general, they are relevant,
     effective and efficient; however, overall system costs could be decreased by shifting service delivery
     toward the beginning of the health care continuum to reduce some of the later, acute care service
     delivery demands.



92   #buildingAlberta                                         GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
               The results-based budgeting review findings of programs and services in the health benefits
               area (drug and supplementary health benefits programs) also indicated the need to improve the
               effectiveness and efficiency of drug and supplementary health benefits programs.
               Integrating and coordinating the programs will enable the Government of Alberta to reduce health
               spending, achieve health system sustainability and improve the health status of Albertans.

               Wellness, Acute and Continuing Care
               The wellness line of business includes programs and services that promote awareness of healthy
               living, support prevention of disease, regulate food safety and ensure fresh drinking water.
               The acute care line of business includes services that provide necessary and active short-term
               treatment of a serious injury or illness, an urgent medical condition, or during recovery from
               surgery. Treatment may require a short stay in an emergency or urgent care centre, admission
               to a hospital, the assistance of diagnostic services, surgery, or follow-up out-patient care in the
               community.
               The continuing care line of business includes programs and services that provide Albertans with
               the health, personal care and accommodation services they need to support their independence
               and quality of life. Continuing care services are provided in three settings: home living, supportive
               living, and facility living.
               Programs and services in these lines of business are scheduled for review in 2013-14. The results-
               based budgeting review process will examine relevance, effectiveness and efficiency in achieving
               overall outcomes.

          2         SECURING ALBERTA’S ECONOMIC FUTURE: Making strategic investments in both human
                    capital and infrastructure to strengthen Alberta, grow our knowledge-inspired economy, and
                    improve Alberta’s competitiveness in the global market place.

               Two of the government’s strategic goals fall under the Securing Alberta’s Economic Future theme:
               ■■   Strategic Goal Four: Invest in Learning
               ■■   Strategic Goal Five: Preserve the Province’s Finances

               In 2012-13, the government continued to build on programs and services aimed at achieving
               economic success and prosperity for Alberta. From advancing research programs in health to high-
               speed interconnectivity for rural Albertans, the government is committed to building a knowledge-
               inspired economy and working toward a strong fiscal future.
STRATEGIC GOAL FOUR. Invest in Learning: Build a knowledge-inspired economy by offering a
sustainable education system that meets the needs of our province and Albertans throughout their lives.

                 2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Alberta Innovates
               Coordination and focus is critical in a research and innovation ecosystem as diverse as Alberta’s.
               This year, the government formed the Research and Innovation System Strategic Integration Team
               (RISIT) – including representatives from the Government of Alberta, Alberta Innovates, and
               Campus Alberta – to streamline and align research and innovation activities in the province. RISIT
               will articulate the common system outcomes and Government of Alberta priorities as they evolve
               in the Alberta Research and Innovation Plan, which in turn guides Alberta Innovates corporations
               and post-secondary research institutions. One example of a research program that focused on
               government priorities is the Collaborative Research and Innovation Opportunities program. This
               program was launched by Alberta Innovates – Health Solutions in 2012-13, bringing together
               experts from diverse disciplines to address complex health problems. Another government research
               priority is to grow capacity in agriculture and energy research. This year, the government built on
               previous investments by providing committed funding for prion research to support

94             #buildingAlberta                                                        GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
               Recruitment of post-secondary students in rural areas, Métis and First Nations communities
               A leadership team was formed to guide activities that address under-representation of groups in
               post-secondary education, including First Nations, Métis and Inuit (FNMI) Albertans. Activities
               included developing an approach for renewable annual funding for FNMI communities to provide
               continued support for adult and family literacy development and delivering workshops in FNMI
               communities that offer culturally-relevant guidance on career and education planning to students,
               teachers and parents.
               Government provided funding to not-for-profit organizations such as Women Building Futures

               High-speed internet connectivity for rural Albertans
               The Final Mile Rural Connectivity Initiative (FMRCI) is the Alberta government’s strategy to
               ensure high-speed Internet access is available to at least 98 per cent of Albertans. In 2012-13,
               government administered the $5 million grant program to assist municipalities, First Nations and
               Métis communities to gain high-speed Internet access. In addition, a satellite program addressing
               the distance costs for rural and remote locations was provided. In 2012-13, 23 community-
               led projects were approved to address broadband service gaps and extend coverage in rural
               communities. Through these programs, the FMRCI has almost reached its 98 per cent goal.
               The Alberta SuperNet connects over 3,800 government buildings, libraries, schools, hospitals
               and municipalities in 402 rural and 27 urban communities province-wide. Over the past year,
               government launched new and upgraded existing stakeholder services for improved speed and
               reliability. The needs of the learning sector were supported by providing high-speed connectivity
               to 19 new schools. Connection consolidations achieved approximately $1 million in annual cost
               savings which were reinvested to meet increased demand for network capacity.

MEASURING UP                                                                                             #buildingAlberta                   95
STRATEGIC GOAL FIVE. Preserve the Province’s Finances: Spend wisely, save intelligently, manage our
finances and improve Alberta’s competitiveness to build a strong fiscal foundation for future Albertans.



               2012–13 Key Accomplishments

             Re-balancing the Fiscal Framework
             A fiscal framework defines the rules and limitations that the province must follow when creating
             a budget. The Alberta government deals with revenue volatility and the related spending issues
             through its fiscal framework. In fall 2012, the Alberta government consulted with Albertans to
             help set the boundaries for a renewed fiscal policy and savings strategy. A report on the public
             consultation titled Dollars and Sense is available on the government’s website at www.alberta.ca.
             A new fiscal framework was introduced in Budget 2013 when the Fiscal Responsibility Act and the
             Government Accountability Act were replaced by the new Fiscal Management Act.

             Results-Based Budgeting (RBB)
             The Results-based Budgeting Act was implemented which commits the government to review all
             programs and services to assess whether they are meeting their intended outcomes efficiently and
             effectively. A report was tabled in November 2012 outlining details of a three-year process. Since
             then, the fiscal situation prompted the government to accelerate RBB. The first full round of three
             cycles will be completed by 2014.

             Labour Productivity
             Labour productivity indicates economic growth, competitiveness, and living standards within the
             economy. It is the measure of labour productivity (and all that this measure takes into account)
             which helps explain the principal economic foundations that are necessary for both economic
             growth and social development.

                 Roads and Highways
                 While the government faces fiscal challenges, the overall economy remains strong. This means that
                 while a thoughtful and measured approach needs to be taken to managing the province’s finances, it
                 is necessary to provide programs, services and infrastructure like roads and highways to support the
                 growing economy and population.
                 In 2012-13, progress was made on the development of an integrated, long-term transportation
                 strategy to direct the future of Alberta’s multi-modal transportation network, encompassing road,
                 rail and air, public transportation, walking and cycling. Innovative financing and funding options
                 are an important part of the strategy.
                 Improvements to Alberta’s provincial highway network were achieved throughout the year to help
                 connect communities and support economic and social growth. Significant progress was made on
                 the ring roads in Edmonton and Calgary, as construction commenced in June 2012 on the final
                 stage of the Northeast section of Anthony Henday Drive with completion scheduled for October
                 2016, and construction continued on bridge and roadway work for the Southeast segment of
                 Calgary’s Stoney Trail with completion scheduled for October 2013.
               Demonstrating the government’s commitment to support economic development and public safety,
               36 kilometers of new twinning of Highway 63 was opened to traffic in 2012-13, and completion

               the section between Grassland and Fort McMurray is scheduled to be finished by the end of 2016.

                 Impaired Driving Legislation
                 While provision of transportation infrastructure to support Alberta’s growing economy and
                 population is critical, ensuring safe use of roads and highways is also important. One of the
                 biggest obstacles to this is impaired driving. Collisions caused by impaired driving are a major
                 cause of accidental death in Alberta. To improve the safety of Alberta’s roadways, the government
                 implemented impaired driving legislation changes. The new legislation has tougher penalties aimed
                 at discouraging this behaviour and encouraging greater personal responsibility when driving. It
                 introduced stronger sanctions for new drivers with blood alcohol content over .00 (Alberta Zero


 MEASURING UP                                                                                          #buildingAlberta                   97
     Alcohol Tolerance Program - Graduated Driver’s Licence) and for drivers with blood alcohol
     content higher than .05. Tougher consequences were also applied to repeat offenders and those with
     blood alcohol content over .08 which is a Criminal Code offence.


          Results-Based Budgeting Lines of Business−Securing Alberta’s Economic Future

     As indicated in Figure 1 on page 81 there are two lines of business that align with the Securing
     Alberta’s Economic Future theme:
     ■■    Education
     ■■    Enterprise and Ministry Support Services

     Education
     This line of business includes programs related to operating support for school boards and post-
     secondary institutions, research and innovation, and support for facilities. It also includes private
     school support, student health services, teachers’ pensions, and supports for adult learning
     including student financial aid.
     Education programs will be examined in the next cycle of RBB in late 2013. The review will focus
     on the themes of equitable, affordable access, ensuring quality, support to maximize individual
     potential and preparedness for life and work and the community.

     Enterprise and Ministry Support Services
     Enterprise Support Services includes programs and services that support administrative activities
     across all ministries, including accommodations, financial services, human resources, information
     technology and legal services.
     Ministry support services include programs and services related to the administration of individual
     government ministries, including ministers’ offices and deputy ministers’ offices.
     In 2012-13, 17 programs in this area were reviewed; those related to enterprise resource planning,
     financial management, information technology, human resources, legal services, employee pension
     management, vehicle fleet management, and other forms of logistical support. Reviews of more
     programs within this line of business will continue through 2013-14.

        3           ADVANCING WORLD-LEADING RESOURCE STEWARDSHIP: Developing our natural

               Two of the government’s strategic goals fall under the Advancing World-leading Resource
               Stewardship theme:
               ■■   Strategic Goal Six: Innovation and Responsible Resource Development
               ■■   Strategic Goal Seven: Build Relationships and Markets

               From continued exploration and implementation of energy efficiencies and alternative energy
               sources to advancing Alberta’s relationships with national and international partners, government is
               committed to establishing and achieving key environmental outcomes while developing its natural
               resources in a responsible manner.

STRATEGIC GOAL SIX. Innovative and Responsible Resource Development: Find innovative solutions to
developing Alberta’s resources while protecting Alberta’s environment to provide a healthy Alberta for future
generations.


                2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Regulatory Enhancement Task Force Recommendations
               The Regulatory Enhancement Task Force made six recommendations. Significant highlights of
               accomplishments related to implementing these recommendations are:
               ■■   Establishing the Policy Management Office (PMO) in September 2012;
               ■■   Development of a PMO access system is underway to help ensure integration of natural resource
                    policies and provide an interface between policy development and assurance, and provide a
                    single source for all Government of Alberta policies related to and affecting the development of
                    oil, gas, oil sands and coal;
               ■■   The Responsible Energy Development Act (REDA), received royal assent on December 10,
                    2012. The Act establishes the Alberta Energy Regulator (AER), which will bring together the
                    regulatory functions from the Energy Resources Conservation Board and Environment and
                    Sustainable Resource Development to begin operations in June 2013;
               ■■   PMO completed three separate consultation sessions – one was a general session for all
                    Albertans, another was with First Nations and a third was with Métis between February and
                    May 2013. Input from all consultation sessions will inform development of regulations to
                    support REDA; and,
               ■■   When proclaimed, Part 3 of REDA will allow registration of private surface agreements with the
                    Alberta Energy Regulator (AER). These registered agreements can be enforced by the AER.
               Additionally, the government continues its commitment to establishing an Integrated Resource
               Management System which will simplify the existing complex processes for future resource
               development and set the environmental, economic and social outcomes that Albertans expect.

               Open Source Information System on Science-based Environmental Indicators
               In October 2012, Alberta announced it will transition to an enhanced environmental monitoring
               system on a provincial scale, beginning in the oil sands region. A new agency will be established to
               lead the enhanced monitoring system.


MEASURING UP                                                                          #buildingAlberta            99
      Alberta’s Environmental Monitoring Agency will have four main responsibilities:
      ■■   It will provide central coordination for monitoring activities in Alberta. Current monitoring
           programs conducted by partners such as the watershed planning and advisory Councils,
           airshed groups, and programs overseen by the Government of Alberta will transition to the
           Environmental Monitoring Agency;
      ■■   It will set standards to integrate all monitoring activities. The Agency will work with partners
           and local stakeholders to establish appropriate parameters and environmental conditions to be
           monitored;
      ■■   It will provide scientific oversight and expertise to ensure credible data is collected. The Agency
           will direct environmental monitoring activities from a science perspective; and,
      ■■   It will provide open access to the data collected and will be the authoritative source of reporting
           environmental information to the public.

      Joint Canada-Alberta Implementation Plan for Oil Sands Monitoring
      The governments of Canada and Alberta released the Joint Canada-Alberta Implementation Plan
      for Oil Sands Monitoring in February 2012. The Joint Plan is focused on regional baseline and
      cumulative effects monitoring as opposed to facility-specific compliance monitoring. Facility-
      specific compliance monitoring will continue to be done by industry.
      The following projects are completed or underway:
      ■■   A tailings pond pilot project to apply various cost-effective and continuous monitoring
           technologies and test the feasibility for tailings ponds emission measurement;
      ■■   An Enhanced Deposition Study which measures air pollutants that fall to the earth in the
           Athabasca Oil Sands Region. Results from this study will help design protocols for monitoring
           and will also develop a baseline to measure the impacts of future development on the oil sands
           region;
      ■■   A project with Environment Canada where remote sensing is being used to monitor air
           pollutants such as nitrogen dioxide and sulphur dioxide from oil sands operations. Data will be
           available in 2013-14; and,
      ■■   An initiative under the general direction of the Integrated Resource Management System to
           ensure that the Environmental Monitoring Agency will support the management frameworks of
           the regional plans by providing data for progress indicators of regional plan outcomes.

      Land-use Regional Plans
      On September 1, 2012 the Lower Athabasca Regional Plan came into effect with the
      implementation of the Air Quality Management Framework; a Surface Water Quality Management
      Framework; and a Regional Groundwater Management Framework with interim limits. There is
      ongoing work to develop Lower Athabasca Regional management frameworks for surface water
      quantity, tailings management and biodiversity management. A regional strategic assessment of
      cumulative effects in the South Athabasca oil sands area in the Lower Athabasca Region is also
      underway.
      Public and stakeholder consultations were held in the fall of 2012 to gather input on
      recommendations made by the Regional Advisory Council for development of the South
      Saskatchewan Regional Plan. The plan is currently being developed and will undergo consultation
      in 2013. Cumulative effects management frameworks for air and water quality are a commitment
      in the plan’s terms of reference.


100   #buildingAlberta                                            GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
               Electric Statutes Amendment Act (formerly Bill 50)
               The Electric Utilities Act was reviewed and amended to remove Cabinet’s authority to approve
               future critical transmission infrastructure and have the Alberta Utilities Commission (AUC)
               approve all new transmission facilities. The Critical Transmission Review Committee (CTRC)
               was established to review the two high-voltage direct current (HVDC) North-South transmission
               facilities. The CTRC, an expert panel, consulted with over 40 organizations in developing its
               recommendations and conclusions. The CTRC recommended proceeding with the development of
               the two 500 kilovolt HVDC transmission lines as soon as possible. The AUC, after extensive public
               hearings, approved the routing of the two lines in 2012-13. Construction of the two lines has now
               commenced.

               Water for Life Strategy
               Public province-wide conversations were held in 20 communities from February to March 2013
               to collect input regarding water with a focus on four priority areas: healthy lakes; water use in
               hydraulic fracturing; drinking water and wastewater systems; and water management. If Albertans
               were unable to take part in the facilitated sessions, opportunities were provided for participation
               including an online survey.
               In addition to the facilitated public sessions, several targeted stakeholder sessions were held in
               communities where Watershed Planning and Advisory Councils already exist. These meetings
               engaged stakeholders with significant interest and involvement in water management. First Nations
               and Métis were engaged on water issues through separate processes.
               External stakeholder engagement was completed on wetland replacement in late 2012 which
               supported the finalization of a Wetland Policy and implementation plan.

               River Water Quality
               The Alberta River Water Quality Index examines a very broad range of water quality parameters,
               many of which tend to fluctuate over time. In 2011-12, four out of six river systems assessed as
               part of the Alberta River Water Quality Index demonstrated good to excellent water quality. A
               certain level of inter-annual variability in Index ratings is expected and typically observed. In April
               of 2011, sampling in the North Saskatchewan River basin coincided with snow melt, which likely
               contributed to increased non-point source runoff to the river. Contaminants (bacteria, nutrients,
               pesticides) transported by this runoff may have caused an increase in the number and magnitude
               of water quality guideline exceedances, resulting in a lower River Water Quality Index rating at the
               downstream monitoring station. Similarly, July rainfall in the Peace River basin likely contributed
               to guideline exceedances for nutrients, metals, bacteria and pesticides and an index rating of “fair”
               at the downstream monitoring station. Various natural and human factors, including flow volume,
               degree of development in the basin, point source effluent discharges, and nonpoint source runoff
               (e.g., from agricultural fields), can influence the River Water Quality Index.




MEASURING UP                                                                            #buildingAlberta           101
               Energy efficiency and sustainability
               Through the On-Farm Energy Management Program, 215 energy efficiency projects were
               completed and funded by the Growing Forward program. This program shares the cost of
               investments that improve energy efficiency on Alberta farms. This enables producers to conserve
               energy and reduce carbon emissions, ultimately reducing the environmental footprint of Alberta’s
               agriculture industry. Over $6 million (grants and producer contributions) was invested in energy
               efficient technologies and practices. The impact to the producer is increased efficiencies and reduced
               production costs. The expected payback on these investments for producers ranged from two to
               seven years based on their energy savings. This corresponds to several tonnes of CO2 offset each year
               for adoption of these technologies.

               Reduction of Carbon Dioxide Emissions
              Alberta communicated with the federal government about Alberta’s electric system and the
              potential economic and policy risks to Alberta associated with the proposed federal coal regulations.
              This dialogue helped influence several changes to the final version of the regulations, released
              September 12, 2012 that will result in reduced impacts on Alberta consumers, the AlbertaMeasuring

STRATEGIC GOAL SEVEN. Build Relationships and Markets: Build relationships with our partners,
enhance market access and attract investment.

                2012–13 Key Accomplishments

               Market Access and Investment
               Premier Redford led missions to Virginia, Washington State, Arizona, Chicago and Washington
               D.C. to promote Alberta as a leader in responsible energy development and to emphasize the
               mutual benefit that a strong energy relationship has on economies on both sides of the border.
               Advocating for expanded market access for Alberta’s oil is a major focus for David Manning, the
               new Alberta envoy appointed to Washington, D.C. The Premier Redford also undertook missions
               to Asia in summer and fall 2012 to foster trade and investment opportunities there.
               Alberta’s new Ottawa office will serve to promote the province and enhance our strong relationship
               with the federal government. The office gives Alberta a strong on-the-ground presence and the
               ability to comment early and regularly on policy developments impacting Albertans. Alan L. Ross
               was appointed Alberta’s representative in Ottawa on February 1, 2013.

               Canadian Energy Strategy
               Alberta is co-leading the development of a Canadian Energy Strategy, with Manitoba and
               Newfoundland and Labrador, to foster Canada’s potential to become a recognized global energy
               leader.
              Developing the Canadian Energy Strategy through the Council of the Federation highlights
              the importance of resource development to the national economy. Provincial and territorial
              jurisdictions across Canada are considering the interconnected nature of resource development
              and the economy. By working together, provinces and territories can create jobs, expand
              their economies, build manufacturing opportunities, and advance research and technological

MEASURING UP                                                                                                  #buildingAlberta                       103
      Alberta’s International Strategy
      A new International Strategy was developed that will guide the activities of government
      internationally. The strategy will be launched in 2013-14.
      Work continued on the implementation of Alberta Abroad, which will place talented post-
      secondary graduates in short-term work placements (externships) in host organizations abroad.
      Host organizations are expected to include international multilateral organizations, governments
      of other provinces, international financial institutions, academic think-tanks, and industry
      associations.

      Aboriginal Consultation
      The Government of Alberta is committed to strengthening relationships with First Nations through
      the continued recognition of the Treaty relationship between First Nations and the Crown.
      Alberta will consult with honour, respect, and good faith, with a view to reconciling First Nations’
      Treaty rights and traditional uses with its mandate to manage provincial Crown lands and resources
      for the benefit of all Albertans.

      Long-term Governance and Funding Arrangements with the Metis Settlements
      On March 12, 2013, Premier Redford, the Minister of Aboriginal Relations and the President of
      the Metis Settlements General Council signed a Long-term Arrangement Agreement. The ten-
      year agreement contains commitments to strengthen Metis Settlement governance, contribute
      $85 million to the long-term sustainability of Metis Settlement communities, and establish a
      fiscal relationship between the province and Metis Settlements comparable to that of other local
      governments.
      The Metis Settlements Amendment Act received royal assent on May 27, 2013. Highlights of the Act
      include a clear division between the roles of the Settlement councils and administration, greater
      transparency in financial reporting and the development of a code-of-conduct for councilors.
      An agreement-in-principle to amend the existing Co-Management Agreement was reached
      which allows for an alternative process for the tenure of oil, gas and oil sands rights on Metis
      Settlement land. This new process, which allows Metis Settlements to become direct participants
      in oil and gas exploration on settlement land, will contribute to long-term sustainability for Metis
      Settlements.	

      Aboriginal Women
      In 2012-13, the Government of Alberta made it a priority to support initiatives that target
      improved opportunities for Aboriginal women in Alberta. To address this priority, over $545,000
      was provided to support 14 projects aimed at improving the social well-being of Aboriginal women
      and supporting Aboriginal women in economic development and entrepreneurship. Projects to
      improve social well-being include the Aboriginal Women’s Professional Association leadership
      training program and the Three Eagles Society program of activities and resources for raising
      awareness to end violence against girls and women. Projects aimed at improving economic well-
      being include the Next Step to Success Capacity Building Program which trains facilitators and
      provides business planning workshops and the Women Building Futures Aboriginal Engagement
      Strategy which provides career planning and skills enhancement workshops.




104   #buildingAlberta                                         GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
                    Results-Based Budgeting Line of Business
                    −Advancing World-leading Resource Stewardship

               As indicated in Figure 1 on page 81 there are two lines of business that align with the Advancing
               World-leading Resource Stewardship theme:
               ■■   Resource Management and Environmental Stewardship
               ■■   Economic Development

               Resource Management and Environmental Stewardship
               This line of business include programs and services supporting management of Alberta’s natural
               resources, including water, fish and wildlife, and public lands. Other major programs include those
               supporting irrigation and quasi-judicial processes such as the Natural Resources Conservation
               Board. Some programs and services help protect the environment and address important issues
               such as climate change.
               The resource management and environmental stewardship line of business will be reviewed during
               the second phase of results-based budgeting in late 2013. The review will include programs
               currently delivered by six government ministries as well as six agencies that have been engaged
               in developing a plan and in completing preliminary activities such as inventories and program
               overviews that will inform the upcoming review.

               Economic Development
               This line of business includes programs and services that enhance market access and encourage
               value-added industry, provide a stable, secure and predictable business climate, and ensure the
               infrastructure and transportation capacity is there to support a growing economy.
               Over 40 programs and services delivered by government were selected for review in 2012‑13 based
               on their connection to the goals of market access, value-added, and diversification. Decisions
               reflected in Budget 2013 are directly aligned with the results of the review process: economic
               development programs and services will continue to benefit from increased coordination,
               collaboration and measurement of results to ensure that all are meeting intended outcomes with an
               optimal use of resources.
               Budget 2013 decisions impacting Alberta Innovates, for example, emphasize the need to strengthen
               relationships between research and practice, encouraging greater translation of research into
               products, services, delivery and policy. Additionally, the government is creating a new umbrella
               organization dedicated to unleashing more robust collaboration through Alberta’s post-secondary
               education, research, innovation and technology commercialization system. The purpose of the
               organization is to further focus the applied research and innovation agenda in Alberta and to
               support the evolution of Alberta’s economy.
               Results of the review further support the need for Alberta to expand its international profile
               and access to markets, particularly in today’s competitive global market place. Through a new
               International Strategy, Alberta will build its international network by putting expertise and
               experience on the ground in key markets.
               The government continues to refine and shape its approach to economic development in the
               province to foster sustainable growth and prosperity.

106          #buildingAlberta   GOVERNMENT OF ALBERTA 2012-13 ANNUAL REPORT
       AUDITOR’S REPORT



       To the Members of the Legislative Assembly


       I have audited the performance measures identified as “audited” in the performance measures by goal
       section of Measuring Up for the year ended March 31, 2013. The audited performance measures are the
       responsibility of the Government of Alberta and are prepared on its behalf by the Ministry of Treasury
       Board and Finance’s management in accordance with the following criteria:
       ■■   Reliability—The information used in applying performance measure methodologies agrees with
            underlying source data for the current and prior years’ results.
       ■■   Understandability—The performance measure methodologies and results are presented clearly.
       ■■   Comparability—The methodologies for performance measure preparation are applied consistently
            for the current and prior years’ results.
       ■■   Completeness—The goals, performance measures and related targets match those included in
            Budget 2012.

       My responsibility is to express an opinion on the reliability, understandability, comparability and
       completeness of these performance measures based on my audit. My audit was not designed to assess the
       relevance and sufficiency of the audited performance measures in demonstrating Government of Alberta
       progress towards the related goals.

       I conducted my audit in accordance with Canadian generally accepted auditing standards. Those
       standards require that I plan and perform an audit to obtain reasonable assurance whether the audited
       performance measures are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis,
       evidence supporting these performance measures. An audit also includes assessing the principles used
       and the significant judgments made by management, as well as evaluating the overall presentation of the
       audited performance measures.

       In my opinion, the audited performance measures in the performance measures by goal section of
       Measuring Up present fairly, in all material respects, results in accordance with the criteria of reliability,
       understandability, comparability, and completeness as described above.


       [Original signed by Merwan N. Saher, FCA]

       Auditor General
       June19, 2013
       Edmonton, AB
